In a circle, if the set of E constraints form a cycle (i.e., they connect all elements), and there is any N constraint, then it's impossible.

Otherwise, if the E constraints form multiple components, then each component can have its own value, and we can assign different values to different components to satisfy the inequalities.

For example, in "ENE" for n=3, the E constraints at 1 and 3 connect a1=a2 and a3=a1, so a1=a2=a3, so they form a cycle (a1=a2 and a3=a1), so all are equal, and there is an inequality between a2 and a3, which is impossible.

In "NEN" for n=3, no E constraint, so three components of size 1, and we can assign different values to satisfy the inequalities.

In "NEE" for n=3: a2=a3 and a3=a1, so a1=a2=a3, and a1≠a2 → impossible.

In "EN" for n=2: the two constraints are on the same pair, so it's a cycle of size 2: a1=a2 from s1, and a2≠a1 from s2, so a1=a2 and a1≠a2 → impossible.

In a circle, the E constraints define connected components. If the entire circle is connected by E constraints, then all values must be equal, and if there is any N constraint, it's impossible.

Otherwise, if the circle is not fully connected by E constraints, then we can assign different values to different components to satisfy the N constraints.

So the algorithm is:

1. Build a graph of the array with n nodes (1 to n)
2. For each i from 0 to n-1 (0-indexed), if s[i] == 'E', add an edge between i and (i+1) mod n
3. Find the connected components of the graph using the E edges
4. For each connected component, if within the component there is an edge with s[i] = 'N', then it's impossible because we cannot have both equality and inequality within the same component.

In the example "ENE" for n=3 (0-indexed: s[0]='E', s[1]='N', s[2]='E'):
- E at 0: edge between 0 and 1
- E at 2: edge between 2 and 0 (since (2+1) mod 3 = 0)
- So edges: 0-1, 0-2
- This connects all three: 0,1,2 are in the same component
- Now, check if there is an N constraint within this component: s[1]='N' between node 1 and 2
- Since 1 and 2 are in the same component, we have an inequality constraint between two nodes that must be equal, which is impossible

In "EN" for n=2 (0-indexed: s[0]='E', s[1]='N'):
- E at 0: edge between 0 and 1
- N at 1: constraint between 1 and 0 (since (1+1) mod 2 = 0)
- The only component is 0-1, and there is an N constraint between 1 and 0, so impossible

In "NENN" for n=4 (0-indexed: s[0]='N', s[1]='E', s[2]='N', s[3]='N'):
- E at 1: edge between 1 and 2
- So component: {1,2}
- Nodes 0,3 are isolated (no E edges)
- Check each component:
  - Component {0}: no internal N constraint (since no edge between nodes in this component) → OK
  - Component {1,2}: has edge between 1 and 2 with s[1]='E' → so no internal N constraint? 
  - What about the N constraints? The N constraints are between (i,i+1) with s[i]='N'
  - s[0]='N' between 0 and 1: 0 and 1 are in different components → no problem
  - s[2]='N' between 2 and 3: 2 and 3 are in different components (2 in {1,2}, 3 alone) → only between different components
  - s[3]='N' between 3 and 0: between different components
- So no internal N constraint within any component → possible

In "ENNEENE" for n=7:
- s = "ENNEENE" (0-indexed)
- E at 0: edge 0-1
- N at 1: no edge
- N at 2: no edge
- E at 3: edge 3-4
- E at 4: edge 4-5
- N at 5: no edge
- E at 6: edge 6-0 (since (6+1) mod 7 = 0)
- So edges: 0-1, 3-4, 4-5, 6-0
- Components:
  - {0,1} because 0-1 and 0-6
  - {3,4,5} because 3-4, 4-5
  - {2} (only node 2)
- Check each component for internal N constraints:
  - {0,1}: has only E edge between 0-1, no N edge within it (N edges are between (1,2), (2,3), (5,6), (6,0))
  - (1,2): between component {0,1} and {2} → different components
  - (2,3): between {2} and {3,4,5} → different
  - (5,6): between {3,4,5} and {0,1} (6 in {0,1}) → different
  - (6,0): within {0,1} → but s[6]='E' → not N
  - So no internal N constraint in any component → possible

Therefore, the algorithm is:

For each test case:
- Read string s of length n
- Build a graph with n nodes (0 to n-1)
- For each i in range(n):
  - if s[i] == 'E', add edge between i and (i+1) % n
- Find connected components of the graph
- For each component, check if there is any pair (j, k) in the component such that there is an N constraint between j and k in the original string
- If such a pair exists, then output "NO", else "YES"

But how to check for this? For each edge that is in a component and is an N constraint, it would be between two nodes in the component.

For each i from 0 to n-1:
- if s[i] == 'N', then the edge between i and (i+1) % n should not be within the same component.

In other words, if for any i with s[i] == 'N', the nodes i and (i+1) % n are in the same connected component (defined by the E edges), then it's impossible.

Otherwise, it's possible.

So the algorithm is:

1. Read t
2. For each test case:
   - Read s (length n)
   - Create a graph with n nodes
   - For each i in range(n):
     - if s[i] == 'E', add edge between i and (i+1) % n
   - Find connected components (using union-find or DFS)
   - For each i in range(n):
     - if s[i] == 'N' and i and (i+1) % n are in the same component, then it's impossible
   - If found, print "NO", else print "YES"

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: "EEE" (n=3)
- E at 0: edge 0-1
- E at 1: edge 1-2
- E at 2: edge 2-0
- Components: one component {0,1,2}
- Check N constraints: none → print "YES" ✓

Example 2: "EN" (n=2)
- E at 0: edge 0-1
- N at 1: between 1 and 0 (since (1+1)%2=0)
- Components: {0,1} (connected by E)
- s[1]='N' and nodes 1 and 0 are in the same component → impossible → "NO" ✓

Example 3: "ENNEENE" (n=7)
- E edges: at 0:0-1, at 3:3-4, at 4:4-5, at 6:6-0
- Components: 
  - 0,1,6 in one component (0-1, 0-6)
  - 3,4,5 in one component (3-4, 4-5)
  - 2 alone
- Check N constraints:
  - i=1: s[1]='N' → between 1 and 2
  - 1 and 2: 1 in {0,1,6}, 2 in {2} → different components → OK
  - i=2: s[2]='N' → between 2 and 3
  - 2 in {2}, 3 in {3,4,5} → different → OK
  - i=5: s[5]='N' → between 5 and 0
  - 5 in {3,4,5}, 0 in {0,1,6} → different → OK
- No internal N constraint → "YES" ✓

Example 4: "NENN" (n=4)
- s = "NENN"
- E at 1: edge 1-2
- Components: {1,2}, {0}, {3}
- N constraints:
  - i=0: s[0]='N' → between 0 and 1 → 0 in {0}, 1 in {1,2} → different → OK
  - i=2: s[2]='N' → between 2 and 3 → 2 in {1,2}, 3 in {3} → different → OK
  - i=3: s[3]='N' → between 3 and 0 → 3 in {3}, 0 in {0} → different → OK
- No internal N constraint → "YES" ✓

Perfect! This matches the expected outputs.

So the solution is to use union-find or DFS to find connected components of the graph defined by E constraints, and then for each N constraint, check if the two nodes are in the same component.

If any N constraint has both nodes in the same component, output "NO", else "YES".

I'll implement this using union-find for simplicity.

```python
def solve():
    t = int(input())
    for _ in range(t):
        s = input().strip()
        n = len(s)
        
        # Union-Find data structure
        parent = list(range(n))
        
        def find(x):
            if parent[x] != x:
                parent[x] = find(parent[x])
            return parent[x]
        
        def union(x, y):
            px, py = find(x), find(y)
            if px != py:
                parent[px] = py
        
        # Add edges for E constraints
        for i in range(n):
            if s[i] == 'E':
                # Connect i and (i+1) % n
                union(i, (i+1) % n)
        
        # Check for any N constraint that has both nodes in the same component
        possible = True
        for i in range(n):
            if s[i] == 'N':
                j = (i+1) % n
                if find(i) == find(j):
                    possible = False
                    break
        
        print("YES" if possible else "NO")

solve()
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You had $n$ positive integers $a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n$ arranged in a circle. For each pair of neighboring numbers ($a_1$ and $a_2$, $a_2$ and $a_3$, ..., $a_{n - 1}$ and $a_n$, and $a_n$ and $a_1$), you wrote down: are the numbers in the pair equal or not.

Unfortunately, you've lost a piece of paper with the array $a$. Moreover, you are afraid that even information about equality of neighboring elements may be inconsistent. So, you are wondering: is there any array $a$ which is consistent with information you have about equality or non-equality of corresponding pairs?


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 1000$) — the number of test cases. Next $t$ cases follow.

The first and only line of each test case contains a non-empty string $s$ consisting of characters E and/or N. The length of $s$ is equal to the size of array $n$ and $2 \le n \le 50$. For each $i$ from $1$ to $n$:

if $s_i =$ E then $a_i$ is equal to $a_{i + 1}$ ($a_n = a_1$ for $i = n$);

if $s_i =$ N then $a_i$ is not equal to $a_{i + 1}$ ($a_n \neq a_1$ for $i = n$).


-----Output-----

For each test case, print YES if it's possible to choose array $a$ that are consistent with information from $s$ you know. Otherwise, print NO.

It can be proved, that if there exists some array $a$, then there exists an array $a$ of positive integers with values less or equal to $10^9$.


-----Examples-----

Input
4
EEE
EN
ENNEENE
NENN
Output
YES
NO
YES
YES


-----Note-----

In the first test case, you can choose, for example, $a_1 = a_2 = a_3 = 5$.

In the second test case, there is no array $a$, since, according to $s_1$, $a_1$ is equal to $a_2$, but, according to $s_2$, $a_2$ is not equal to $a_1$.

In the third test case, you can, for example, choose array $a = [20, 20, 4, 50, 50, 50, 20]$.

In the fourth test case, you can, for example, choose $a = [1, 3, 3, 7]$.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

We have a circular array of positive integers, and we know whether each pair of adjacent elements (including the last and first) are equal (E) or not equal (N).

The key insight is that we need to determine if there exists any consistent array that satisfies all the given equality/inequality constraints.

1. **Contradiction in equality constraints**: If we have E (equal) between positions i and i+1, and E between i+1 and i+2, then a_i = a_{i+1} = a_{i+2}, which is consistent.

2. **Contradiction in inequality constraints**: If we have N (not equal) between i and i+1, and N between i+1 and i+2, that's fine as long as a_i ≠ a_{i+1} and a_{i+1} ≠ a_{i+2}.

3. **The critical issue**: The main contradiction happens when we have a cycle with forced equalities that create a contradiction with no equalities.

Let me consider the case where we have an E constraint between consecutive elements. This creates a "chain" of equalities.

If we have a chain of E constraints, then all elements in that chain must be equal.

The key insight is that if we have a cycle of E constraints, we need to check if any N constraints force a contradiction.

Actually, let's think differently. Consider the positions where we have N constraints. For a solution to exist, we cannot have a situation where we're forced to have equal elements by E constraints, but then forced to have unequal elements by N constraints in a cycle.

The key insight is that if we have only E constraints, it's always possible (set all elements equal).

The real issue is when we have both E and N constraints, and they create a contradiction in the cycle.

Specifically, if there's a chain of E constraints that connects two positions, and we have an N constraint between those two positions, that's impossible.

- In a cycle, if we have only E constraints, it's always possible.
- In a cycle, if we have only N constraints, it's possible (just alternate values, like 1, 2, 1, 2, ...).

The real contradiction occurs when we have both E and N constraints that create a cycle where elements must be equal and unequal simultaneously.

Actually, let's think systematically:

In a circular array, if we have a sequence of E constraints, they create connected components of equal values. Elements in the same connected component must have the same value.

If we have an N constraint between two elements in the same connected component, that's impossible.

So the algorithm:
1. Group positions that must be equal (using E constraints)
2. For each N constraint, check if it's between two elements in the same group

All constraints satisfied → YES

So the algorithm is:
1. Use union-find or simply merge groups for E constraints
2. For each N constraint, check if the two positions are in the same group
3. If any N constraint has two positions in the same group, return NO; otherwise YES

## Implementation

I'll implement union-find or use DFS to track groups, but for simplicity, I can use a list to track the group each position belongs to.

Actually, since the constraints are small (n ≤ 50), I can just use a simple union approach.

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    n = len(s)
    
    # Initially, each position is in its own group
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # Process each E constraint
    for i in range(n):
        if s[i] == 'E':
            # a_i equals a_{i+1} (with a_n = a_0)
            j = (i + 1) % n
            union(i, j)
    
    # Check each N constraint
    for i in range(n):
        if s[i] == 'N':
            j = (i + 1) % n
            if find(i) == find(j):
                return "NO"
    
    return "YES"

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

- s[0] = 'E': union(0,1)
- s[1] = 'N': check if find(1) == find(0) → since they're unioned, find(1) == find(0) → return "NO" → correct!

Example 1: "EEE" (n=3)
- s[0]='E': union(0,1)
- s[1]='E': union(1,2) → now 0,1,2 all connected
- s[2]='E': union(2,0) → already connected
- Check N constraints: none → return "YES" → correct!

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a 0-indexed integer array nums and a positive integer x.
You are initially at position 0 in the array and you can visit other positions according to the following rules:

If you are currently in position i, then you can move to any position j such that i < j.
For each position i that you visit, you get a score of nums[i].
If you move from a position i to a position j and the parities of nums[i] and nums[j] differ, then you lose a score of x.

Return the maximum total score you can get.
Note that initially you have nums[0] points.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [2,3,6,1,9,2], x = 5
Output: 13
Explanation: We can visit the following positions in the array: 0 -> 2 -> 3 -> 4.
The corresponding values are 2, 6, 1 and 9. Since the integers 6 and 1 have different parities, the move 2 -> 3 will make you lose a score of x = 5.
The total score will be: 2 + 6 + 1 + 9 - 5 = 13.

Example 2:

Input: nums = [2,4,6,8], x = 3
Output: 20

## Problem Understanding

1. We start at position 0 with score `nums[0]`
2. We can move from position `i` to any position `j` where `i < j`
3. We get `nums[i]` points for visiting position `i`
4. If we move from position `i` to `j` and `nums[i]` and `nums[j]` have different parities (one even, one odd), we lose `x` points
5. We want to maximize total score

## Key Insights

1. We start at position 0, so we always get `nums[0]` points
2. The parity (even/odd) of the current position matters when we move to the next position
3. We can use dynamic programming where we track the maximum score we can achieve ending at each position with a specific parity
4. At each position, we can either skip it or extend from previous positions

- `dp_even`: maximum score achievable ending with an even number
- `dp_odd`: maximum score achievable ending with an odd number

- When we move from one position to another, if parities differ, we lose `x` points
- We can visit positions in any order as long as they are increasing in index

4. Move from odd to odd (no loss)

- `dp_even`: maximum score achievable when the last visited position has an even value
- `dp_odd`: maximum score achievable when the last visited position has an odd value

- If `nums[i]` is even: we can come from any previous position with odd value (lose x), or from a position with even value (no loss)
  - `dp_even = max(dp_even + nums[i], dp_odd - x + nums[i])`
- If `nums[i]` is odd: we can come from any previous position with even value (lose x), or from a position with odd value (no loss)

- `dp_odd`: maximum total score achievable when the last visited position has an odd value

- If nums[0] is odd: dp_odd = nums[0], dp_even = -inf

- If nums[i] is even:

  - So dp_even = max(dp_even, dp_odd - x + nums[i])
- If nums[i] is odd:
  - We can extend from an even previous position (lose x): new_score = dp_even - x + nums[i]
  - We can extend from an odd previous position (no loss): new_score = dp_odd + nums[i]
  - So dp_odd = max(dp_odd, dp_even - x + nums[i])

Actually, the transition should be: when we visit position i, we can either:
1. Start a new sequence from position i (but we can't skip previous positions since we must maintain increasing indices)
2. Extend from any previous position

Since we must visit positions in increasing order, we can only extend from previous positions.

```python
class Solution:
    def maxScore(self, nums: List[int], x: int) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        
        # dp_even: maximum score achievable ending with an even number
        # dp_odd: maximum score achievable ending with an odd number
        dp_even = 0
        dp_odd = 0
        
        # Start with position 0
        if nums[0] % 2 == 0:
            dp_even = nums[0]
        else:
            dp_odd = nums[0]
        
        # Process positions from 1 to n-1
        for i in range(1, n):
            if nums[i] % 2 == 0:
                # Current number is even
                # Can come from previous odd position (lose x) or from previous even position (no loss)
                new_dp_even = max(dp_even + nums[i], dp_odd - x + nums[i])
                dp_even = new_dp_even
            else:
                # Current number is odd
                # Can come from previous even position (lose x) or from previous odd position (no loss)
                new_dp_odd = max(dp_odd + nums[i], dp_even - x + nums[i])
                dp_odd = new_dp_odd
        
        # Return the maximum of both states
        return max(dp_even, dp_odd)
```

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We start at position 0 with score `nums[0]`
2. We can move from position `i` to any position `j` where `i < j`
3. Each position we visit gives us `nums[i]` points
4. When moving from position `i` to `j`, if `nums[i]` and `nums[j]` have different parities (one even, one odd), we lose `x` points
5. We want to maximize the total score

## Key Insights

1. Since we can only move forward (i < j), we process elements from left to right
2. The cost of moving depends only on the parity (even/odd) of the current and next positions
3. We need to decide whether to visit each subsequent position or skip it
4. The decision should consider both the value of the element and the potential penalty for different parities

## Approach

This is a dynamic programming problem where we need to track the maximum score we can achieve while considering parity.

Let's think about states:
- At each position, we can have a state representing the maximum score ending with an even number or an odd number
- When we encounter a new element, we can either skip it or visit it
- If we visit it, we add its value, and if the parity differs from our current ending state, we subtract `x`

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Initialize our DP states: 
   - `max_even` = score ending with an even number
   - `max_odd` = score ending with an odd number
   - Initially, we start at position 0, so if `nums[0]` is even, `max_even = nums[0]`, else `max_odd = nums[0]`

2. For each subsequent position `i` from 1 to n-1:
   - We can choose to visit `nums[i]`
   - If `nums[i]` is even, we can:
     - Continue from a previous even state: `max_even + nums[i]`
     - Continue from a previous odd state: `max_odd + nums[i] - x` (because parity changes)
   - If `nums[i]` is odd, we can:
     - Continue from a previous odd state: `max_odd + nums[i]`
     - Continue from a previous even state: `max_even + nums[i] - x` (because parity changes)

3. At each step, we update our max_even and max_odd based on the best options

## Implementation

```python
class Solution:
    def maxScore(self, nums: List[int], x: int) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        
        # Initialize with the first element
        if nums[0] % 2 == 0:
            max_even = nums[0]
            max_odd = 0
        else:
            max_even = 0
            max_odd = nums[0]
        
        # Process from position 1 to n-1
        for i in range(1, n):
            current_val = nums[i]
            current_parity = current_val % 2
            
            if current_parity == 0:  # current value is even
                # Option 1: continue from even state (no penalty)
                new_even = max_even + current_val
                # Option 2: continue from odd state (penalty of x)
                new_even_from_odd = max_odd + current_val - x
                max_even = max(new_even, new_even_from_odd)
                
                # Update odd state - we can only get odd from previous odd state (no change or continuation)
                # Actually, we can continue from odd state to even, but odd state might not be updated
                # The odd state should remain as it was, because we're not ending in odd
                # However, we can still have odd state from previous odd state
                # Actually, we need to maintain both states independently
                # We only update even state when we add even number
                # For odd state, it remains unchanged
                # But we need to consider if we can extend odd state to odd number
                # The odd state update should be: max_odd remains same because we're adding even
                # Actually, no - we're not creating a new odd state when adding an even
                # The odd state should only be updated when we add an odd number
                # So we don't update max_odd here
                pass
            else:  # current value is odd
                # Option 1: continue from odd state (no penalty)
                new_odd = max_odd + current_val
                # Option 2: continue from even state (penalty of x)
                new_odd_from_even = max_even + current_val - x
                max_odd = max(new_odd, new_odd_from_even)
                
                # Update even state - remains unchanged since we're adding odd
                # So we don't update max_even here
        
        return max(max_even, max_odd)
```

```python
class Solution:
    def maxScore(self, nums: List[int], x: int) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        
        # Initialize with the first element
        if nums[0] % 2 == 0:
            max_even = nums[0]
            max_odd = 0
        else:
            max_even = 0
            max_odd = nums[0]
        
        # Process from position 1 to n-1
        for i in range(1, n):
            current_val = nums[i]
            current_parity = current_val % 2
            
            if current_parity == 0:  # current value is even
                # We can either continue from even state (no penalty)
                # or from odd state (penalty of x)
                new_even = max(max_even + current_val, max_odd + current_val - x)
                # The odd state remains unchanged because we're not creating a new odd ending
                max_even = new_even
            else:  # current value is odd
                # We can either continue from odd state (no penalty)
                # or from even state (penalty of x)
                new_odd = max(max_odd + current_val, max_even + current_val - x)
                # The even state remains unchanged
                max_odd = new_odd
        
        return max(max_even, max_odd)
```

2. We can move from position i to any position j where i < j
3. We get `nums[i]` points when visiting position i

3. We need to decide whether to include each position or skip it
4. For each position, we need to track the maximum score we can achieve ending with an even or odd number

## Strategy

This is a dynamic programming problem where at each position, we maintain two states:
- `even_max`: maximum score achievable when ending at an even number
- `odd_max`: maximum score achievable when ending at an odd number

For each position `i`:
- If `nums[i]` is even:
  - We can extend from an even number: `even_max = max(even_max, odd_max + nums[i] - x)` (switching from odd to even)
  - We can start from scratch: `even_max = max(even_max, nums[i])`
- If `nums[i]` is odd:
  - We can extend from an odd number: `odd_max = max(odd_max, even_max + nums[i] - x)` (switching from even to odd)
  - We can start from scratch: `odd_max = max(odd_max, nums[i])`

- If nums[i] is odd: we can reach it from an even number (paying cost x) or from an odd number (no cost)

```python
class Solution:
    def maxScore(self, nums: List[int], x: int) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        
        # Start with position 0
        # Track maximum score when ending with even number
        # Track maximum score when ending with odd number
        even_max = nums[0] if nums[0] % 2 == 0 else float('-inf')
        odd_max = nums[0] if nums[0] % 2 == 1 else float('-inf')
        
        # Process positions from 1 to n-1
        for i in range(1, n):
            if nums[i] % 2 == 0:  # even number
                # We can reach this even number from:
                # 1. An even number (no cost) - just add nums[i]
                # 2. An odd number (cost x) - plus nums[i] minus x
                new_even = max(even_max + nums[i], odd_max + nums[i] - x)
                # Update even_max
                even_max = new_even
                # Keep odd_max unchanged
            else:  # odd number
                # We can reach this odd number from:
                # 1. An odd number (no cost) - just add nums[i]
                # 2. An even number (cost x) - plus nums[i] minus x
                new_odd = max(odd_max + nums[i], even_max + nums[i] - x)
                odd_max = new_odd
        
        # Return the maximum score between even and odd end states
        return max(even_max, odd_max)
```

Let me trace through Example 1:
- nums = [2,3,6,1,9,2], x = 5
- Position 0: nums[0] = 2 (even)
  - even_max = 2, odd_max = -inf
- Position 1: nums[1] = 3 (odd)
  - new_odd = max(-inf + 3, 2 + 3 - 5) = max(-inf, 0) = 0
  - odd_max = 0
- Position 2: nums[2] = 6 (even)
  - new_even = max(2 + 6, 0 + 6 - 5) = max(8, 1) = 8
  - even_max = 8
- Position 3: nums[3] = 1 (odd)
  - new_odd = max(0 + 1, 8 + 1 - 5) = max(1, 4) = 4
  - odd_max = 4
- Position 4: nums[4] = 9 (odd)
  - new_odd = max(4 + 9, 8 + 9 - 5) = max(13, 12) = 13
  - odd_max = 13
- Position 5: nums[5] = 2 (even)
  - new_even = max(8 + 2, 13 + 2 - 5) = max(10, 10) = 10
  - even_max = 10
- Result: max(10, 13) = 13 ✓

This matches the expected output!

The solution works because:
- We track the maximum score we can achieve ending with an even or odd number
- When we see a new number, we consider both possibilities (same parity - no cost, different parity - cost x)
- We always take the maximum of the two options
- The final answer is the maximum between even and odd end states

Time complexity: O(n)
Space complexity: O(1)

```python
class Solution:
    def maxScore(self, nums: List[int], x: int) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        
        # Start with position 0
        # Track maximum score when ending with even number
        # Track maximum score when ending with odd number
        even_max = nums[0] if nums[0] % 2 == 0 else float('-inf')
        odd_max = nums[0] if nums[0] % 2 == 1 else float('-inf')
        
        # Process positions from 1 to n-1
        for i in range(1, n):
            if nums[i] % 2 == 0:  # even number
                # We can reach this even number from:
                # 1. An even number (no cost) - just add nums[i]
                # 2. An odd number (cost x) - plus nums[i] minus x
                new_even = max(even_max + nums[i], odd_max + nums[i] - x)
                even_max = new_even
            else:  # odd number
                # We can reach this odd number from:
                # 1. An odd number (no cost) - just add nums[i]
                # 2. An even number (cost x) - plus nums[i] minus x
                new_odd = max(odd_max + nums[i], even_max + nums[i] - x)
                odd_max = new_odd
        
        # Return the maximum score between even and odd end states
        return max(even_max, odd_max)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a 0-indexed integer array nums and a positive integer x.
You are initially at position 0 in the array and you can visit other positions according to the following rules:

If you are currently in position i, then you can move to any position j such that i < j.
For each position i that you visit, you get a score of nums[i].
If you move from a position i to a position j and the parities of nums[i] and nums[j] differ, then you lose a score of x.

Return the maximum total score you can get.
Note that initially you have nums[0] points.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [2,3,6,1,9,2], x = 5
Output: 13
Explanation: We can visit the following positions in the array: 0 -> 2 -> 3 -> 4.
The corresponding values are 2, 6, 1 and 9. Since the integers 6 and 1 have different parities, the move 2 -> 3 will make you lose a score of x = 5.
The total score will be: 2 + 6 + 1 + 9 - 5 = 13.

Example 2:

Input: nums = [2,4,6,8], x = 3
Output: 20
Explanation: All the integers in the array have the same parities, so we can visit all of them without losing any score.
The total score is: 2 + 4 + 6 + 8 = 20.

 
Constraints:

2 <= nums.length <= 10^5
1 <= nums[i], x <= 10^6### Format: You will use the following starter code to write the solution to the problem and enclose your code within delimiters.
```python
class Solution:
    def maxScore(self, nums: List[int], x: int) -> int:
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We start at position 0 and can only move to positions with higher indices
2. We get `nums[i]` score for visiting position i
3. When moving from position i to j, if `nums[i]` and `nums[j]` have different parities (one even, one odd), we lose `x` points
4. We want to maximize the total score

## Key Insights

1. We can only move forward (i < j)
2. The parity loss only occurs when we switch from even to odd or odd to even
3. We need to decide which positions to visit optimally

## Strategy

This is a dynamic programming problem where we need to track the maximum score ending with either an even or odd number.

Let's define:
- `dp_even`: maximum score achievable ending with an even number
- `dp_odd`: maximum score achievable ending with an odd number

At each position i, we have two choices:
1. Visit position i and extend the path ending with even/odd number
2. Start a new path from position i

But actually, we need to think about it differently. Since we start at position 0, and we can only move forward, we can process positions sequentially.

For each position i, we have two possibilities:
- We can continue a path that ended with an even number
- We can continue a path that ended with an odd number

When we visit position i:
- If `nums[i]` is even, we can reach it from any previous position, and we only pay the cost if we switch from odd to even
- If `nums[i]` is odd, we can reach it from any previous position, and we only pay the cost if we switch from even to odd

Therefore, we can maintain:
- `max_score_even`: maximum score achievable when the last visited position has even value
- `max_score_odd`: maximum score achievable when the last visited position has odd value

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Initialize `max_score_even` and `max_score_odd` with the value at position 0
2. For each subsequent position i from 1 to n-1:
   - If `nums[i]` is even:
     - We can get to it from any path ending with odd, paying cost x (if we switch from odd to even)
     - So `new_even = max(max_score_even + nums[i], max_score_odd + nums[i] - x)`
     - Update `max_score_even = new_even`
   - If `nums[i]` is odd:
     - We can get to it from any path ending with even, paying cost x (if we switch from even to odd)
     - So `new_odd = max(max_score_odd + nums[i], max_score_even + nums[i] - x)`
     - Update `max_score_odd = new_odd`

Wait, let me reconsider this. Actually, we don't need to pay x when coming from the same parity. We pay x only when switching parities.

So the recurrence is:
- For even `nums[i]`: `new_even = max(max_score_even + nums[i], max_score_odd + nums[i] - x)`
- For odd `nums[i]`: `new_odd = max(max_score_odd + nums[i], max_score_even + nums[i] - x)`

But actually, we don't have to pay x if we're continuing from the same parity. The cost x is only incurred when switching parities.

So the correct approach is:
- `max_even` = maximum score ending with even number
- `max_odd` = maximum score ending with odd number

For each position i:
- If `nums[i]` is even:
  - We can extend from an even path: `max_even + nums[i]`
  - We can extend from an odd path, paying x: `max_odd + nums[i] - x`
  - So new `max_even = max(max_even + nums[i], max_odd + nums[i] - x)`
- If `nums[i]` is odd:
  - We can extend from an odd path: `max_odd + nums[i]`
  - We can extend from an even path, paying x: `max_even + nums[i] - x`
  - So new `max_odd = max(max_odd + nums[i], max_even + nums[i] - x)`

## Edge Cases

- Position 0 is always included (we start there)
- We don't have to visit all positions - we can skip some

## Implementation

```python
class Solution:
    def maxScore(self, nums: List[int], x: int) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        
        # Start with position 0
        if nums[0] % 2 == 0:
            max_even = nums[0]
            max_odd = 0
        else:
            max_even = 0
            max_odd = nums[0]
        
        # Process positions from 1 to n-1
        for i in range(1, n):
            if nums[i] % 2 == 0:  # even number
                # Can extend from even path (no cost) or from odd path (pay x)
                new_even = max(max_even + nums[i], max_odd + nums[i] - x)
                max_even = new_even
                max_odd = max_odd  # odd remains unchanged
            else:  # odd number
                # Can extend from odd path (no cost) or from even path (pay x)
                new_odd = max(max_odd + nums[i], max_even + nums[i] - x)
                max_odd = new_odd
                max_even = max_even  # even remains unchanged
        
        return max(max_even, max_odd)
```

  - new_odd = max(0+3, 2+3-5) = max(3, 0) = 3

  - max_odd = 4, max_even = 8
- i=4: nums[4] = 9 (odd)
  - new_odd = max(4+9, 8+9-5) = max(13, 12) = 13
  - max_odd = 13, max_even = 8
- i=5: nums[5] = 2 (even)
  - new_even = max(8+2, 13+2-5) = max(10, 10) = 10
  - max_even = 10, max_odd = 13

Final answer: max(10, 13) = 13 ✓

- i=2: nums[2]=6 (even) → new_even = max(6+6, 0+6-3) = max(12,3) = 12
- i=3: nums[3]=8 (even) → new_even = max(12+8, 0+8-3) = max(20,5) = 20
- Final answer: 20 ✓

```python
class Solution:
    def maxScore(self, nums: List[int], x: int) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        
        # Start with position 0
        if nums[0] % 2 == 0:
            max_even = nums[0]
            max_odd = 0
        else:
            max_even = 0
            max_odd = nums[0]
        
        # Process positions from 1 to n-1
        for i in range(1, n):

                # Can extend from even path (no cost) or from odd path (pay x)
                new_even = max(max_even + nums[i], max_odd + nums[i] - x)
                max_even = new_even
                max_odd = max_odd  # odd remains unchanged
            else:  # odd number
                # Can extend from odd path (no cost) or from even path (pay x)
                new_odd = max(max_odd + nums[i], max_even + nums[i] - x)
                max_odd = new_odd
                max_even = max_even  # even remains unchanged
        
        return max(max_even, max_odd)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We start at position 0 with score `nums[0]`
2. We can move from position `i` to any position `j` where `i < j`
3. When moving from position `i` to `j`, if `nums[i]` and `nums[j]` have different parities (one even, one odd), we lose `x` points
4. We want to maximize the total score (sum of visited elements minus penalty for parity changes)

## Key Insights

1. Since we can only move forward (i < j), we process the array from left to right
2. The penalty only occurs when we switch between even and odd numbers
3. We need to decide whether to visit each subsequent element or skip it
4. At each position, we need to track the maximum score we can achieve when ending with an even number and when ending with an odd number

## Dynamic Programming Approach

Let's maintain two states:
- `even_score`: maximum score achievable when the last visited position has an even number
- `odd_score`: maximum score achievable when the last visited position has an odd number

At each position `i`:
- If `nums[i]` is even:
  - We can extend from an even position: `even_score + nums[i]`
  - We can extend from an odd position: `odd_score + nums[i] - x` (paying penalty for parity change)
  - Update `even_score = max(even_score + nums[i], odd_score + nums[i] - x)`
- If `nums[i]` is odd:
  - We can extend from an odd position: `odd_score + nums[i]`
  - We can extend from an even position: `even_score + nums[i] - x` (paying penalty for parity change)
  - Update `odd_score = max(odd_score + nums[i], even_score + nums[i] - x)`

Special case: at position 0, we start with `nums[0]`, so if it's even, `even_score = nums[0]`, if odd, `odd_score = nums[0]`.

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
class Solution:
    def maxScore(self, nums: List[int], x: int) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        
        # Initialize scores for even and odd endings
        # We start at position 0, so we have nums[0] as our initial score
        if nums[0] % 2 == 0:
            even_score = nums[0]
            odd_score = 0
        else:
            even_score = 0
            odd_score = nums[0]
        
        # Process positions from 1 to n-1
        for i in range(1, n):
            if nums[i] % 2 == 0:
                # Current number is even
                # Option 1: extend from even position (no penalty)
                new_even = even_score + nums[i]
                # Option 2: extend from odd position (pay penalty x)
                new_even = max(new_even, odd_score + nums[i] - x)
                even_score = new_even
                # odd_score remains unchanged
            else:
                # Current number is odd
                # Option 1: extend from odd position (no penalty)
                new_odd = odd_score + nums[i]
                # Option 2: extend from even position (pay penalty x)
                new_odd = max(new_odd, even_score + nums[i] - x)
                odd_score = new_odd
        
        # Return the maximum of even and odd scores
        return max(even_score, odd_score)
```

## Trace through Example 1

`nums = [2,3,6,1,9,2], x = 5`

- Position 0: nums[0] = 2 (even) → even_score = 2, odd_score = 0
- Position 1: nums[1] = 3 (odd) → 
  - From even: 2 + 3 - 5 = 0
  - From odd: 0 + 3 = 3
  - odd_score = max(3, 0) = 3
- Position 2: nums[2] = 6 (even) → 
  - From even: 2 + 6 = 8
  - From odd: 3 + 6 - 5 = 4
  - even_score = max(8, 4) = 8
- Position 3: nums[3] = 1 (odd) → 
  - From even: 8 + 1 - 5 = 4
  - From odd: 3 + 1 = 4
  - odd_score = max(4, 4) = 4
- Position 4: nums[4] = 9 (odd) → 
  - From even: 8 + 9 - 5 = 12
  - From odd: 4 + 9 = 13
  - odd_score = max(13, 12) = 13
- Position 5: nums[5] = 2 (even) → 
  - From even: 8 + 2 = 10
  - From odd: 13 + 2 - 5 = 10
  - even_score = max(10, 10) = 10

Final answer: max(10, 13) = 13 ✓

The solution correctly handles the parity penalties and finds the optimal path. Time complexity is O(n) and space complexity is O(1).

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We start at position 0 with score `nums[0]`
2. We can move from position `i` to any position `j` where `i < j`
3. We get `nums[i]` points for visiting each position
4. If we move from position `i` to `j` and `nums[i]` and `nums[j]` have different parities (one even, one odd), we lose `x` points
5. We want to maximize the total score

## Key Insights

1. The cost only occurs when we switch between even and odd numbers
2. We can think of this as: we start with `nums[0]`, then for each subsequent position, we either:
   - Add `nums[j]` (without penalty if same parity as previous)
   - Add `nums[j] - x` (with penalty if different parity)
3. We need to track the maximum score we can achieve at each position, considering the parity of the last number we visited

## Approach

We can use dynamic programming with state tracking:
- Keep track of the maximum score we can achieve ending with an even number
- Keep track of the maximum score we can achieve ending with an odd number

However, we can simplify this: for each position, we have two states:
- `even_max`: maximum score achievable when the last visited number is even
- `odd_max`: maximum score achievable when the last visited number is odd

For each new number `nums[i]`:
- If `nums[i]` is even:
  - We can extend from any previous even position: `even_max + nums[i]`
  - We can extend from any previous odd position with penalty: `odd_max + nums[i] - x`
- If `nums[i]` is odd:
  - We can extend from any previous odd position: `odd_max + nums[i]`
  - We can extend from any previous even position with penalty: `even_max + nums[i] - x`

We update our states accordingly and take the maximum.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Initialize `even_max` and `odd_max` to negative infinity
2. Start with position 0: if `nums[0]` is even, set `even_max = nums[0]`, otherwise `odd_max = nums[0]`
3. For each subsequent position i (from 1 to n-1):
   - Calculate new even_max: max(existing even_max + nums[i], existing odd_max + nums[i] - x)
   - Calculate new odd_max: max(existing odd_max + nums[i], existing even_max + nums[i] - x)
4. Return the maximum of `even_max` and `odd_max`

```python
class Solution:
    def maxScore(self, nums: List[int], x: int) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        
        # Initialize with the first element
        # even_max: maximum score ending with even number
        # odd_max: maximum score ending with odd number
        if nums[0] % 2 == 0:
            even_max = nums[0]
            odd_max = 0
        else:
            even_max = 0
            odd_max = nums[0]
        
        # Process remaining positions
        for i in range(1, n):
            current = nums[i]
            new_even_max = even_max + current
            new_odd_max = odd_max + current
            
            # If current number is even, we can come from odd with penalty
            if current % 2 == 0:
                new_even_max = max(new_even_max, odd_max + current - x)
            # If current number is odd, we can come from even with penalty
            else:
                new_odd_max = max(new_odd_max, even_max + current - x)
            
            even_max = new_even_max
            odd_max = new_odd_max
        
        return max(even_max, odd_max)
```

## Trace through Example 1:
- nums = [2,3,6,1,9,2], x = 5
- i=0: nums[0]=2 (even) → even_max=2, odd_max=0
- i=1: nums[1]=3 (odd) → new_odd_max = max(0+3, 2+3-5) = max(3, 0) = 3 → even_max=2, odd_max=3
- i=2: nums[2]=6 (even) → new_even_max = max(2+6, 3+6-5) = max(8, 4) = 8 → even_max=8, odd_max=3
- i=3: nums[3]=1 (odd) → new_odd_max = max(3+1, 8+1-5) = max(4, 4) = 4 → even_max=8, odd_max=4
- i=4: nums[4]=9 (odd) → new_odd_max = max(4+9, 8+9-5) = max(13, 12) = 13 → even_max=8, odd_max=13
- i=5: nums[5]=2 (even) → new_even_max = max(8+2, 13+2-5) = max(10, 10) = 10 → even_max=10, odd_max=13
- Result: max(10, 13) = 13 ✓

The solution correctly handles the parity switching penalty and returns the maximum score.

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a 0-indexed integer array nums and a positive integer x.
You are initially at position 0 in the array and you can visit other positions according to the following rules:

If you are currently in position i, then you can move to any position j such that i < j.
For each position i that you visit, you get a score of nums[i].
If you move from a position i to a position j and the parities of nums[i] and nums[j] differ, then you lose a score of x.

Return the maximum total score you can get.
Note that initially you have nums[0] points.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [2,3,6,1,9,2], x = 5
Output: 13
Explanation: We can visit the following positions in the array: 0 -> 2 -> 3 -> 4.
The corresponding values are 2, 6, 1 and 9. Since the integers 6 and 1 have different parities, the move 2 -> 3 will make you lose a score of x = 5.
The total score will be: 2 + 6 + 1 + 9 - 5 = 13.

Example 2:

Input: nums = [2,4,6,8], x = 3
Output: 20
Explanation: All the integers in the array have the same parities, so we can visit all of them without losing any score.
The total score is: 2 + 4 + 6 + 8 = 20.

 
Constraints:

2 <= nums.length <= 10^5
1 <= nums[i], x <= 10^6### Format: You will use the following starter code to write the solution to the problem and enclose your code within delimiters.

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We start at position 0 with score `nums[0]`
2. We can only move forward (from position i to j where i < j)
3. We get `nums[i]` points for visiting position i
4. When moving from position i to j, if `nums[i]` and `nums[j]` have different parities (one even, one odd), we lose `x` points
5. Goal: maximize total score

## Key Insights

1. We want to visit as many positions as possible to maximize the sum of values
2. The penalty only occurs when moving between different parities
3. We can use dynamic programming where we track the maximum score we can achieve while ending at positions with even or odd values

## Approach

We can use dynamic programming with two states:
- `even_score`: maximum score when the last visited position has even value
- `odd_score`: maximum score when the last visited position has odd value

For each position i (starting from 1), we update:
- If `nums[i]` is even:
  - We can extend from any previous position (even or odd)
  - If we come from odd position: new score = `odd_score + nums[i] - x`
  - If we come from even position: new score = `even_score + nums[i]`
  - Take the maximum of these two options
- If `nums[i]` is odd:
  - We can extend from any previous position (even or odd)
  - If we come from even position: new score = `even_score + nums[i] - x`
  - If we come from odd position: new score = `odd_score + nums[i]`
  - Take the maximum of these two options

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Initialize `even_score` and `odd_score` with `nums[0]` if nums[0] is even/odd respectively
2. Iterate through positions 1 to n-1
3. For each position, update the scores based on the parity of the current value
4. Return the maximum of the two final scores

```python
class Solution:
    def maxScore(self, nums: List[int], x: int) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        
        # Initialize with first position (index 0)
        if nums[0] % 2 == 0:
            even_score = nums[0]
            odd_score = 0
        else:
            even_score = 0
            odd_score = nums[0]
        
        # Process positions from 1 to n-1
        for i in range(1, n):
            current = nums[i]
            if current % 2 == 0:  # current is even
                # Can come from even position (no penalty) or odd position (penalty x)
                new_even_score = max(even_score + current, odd_score + current - x)
                # Update odd_score (it stays the same since we're not adding even)
                # But we need to consider if odd_score should be updated too
                # Actually, we only update the even_score for even numbers
                # The odd_score remains unchanged from previous iteration
                even_score = new_even_score
            else:  # current is odd
                # Can come from odd position (no penalty) or even position (penalty x)
                new_odd_score = max(odd_score + current, even_score + current - x)
                odd_score = new_odd_score
        
        return max(even_score, odd_score)
```

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We start at position 0 with score `nums[0]`
2. We can move from position i to any position j where i < j
3. We get `nums[i]` points for visiting position i
4. If we move from position i to j and the parities (odd/even) of `nums[i]` and `nums[j]` differ, we lose `x` points
5. We want to maximize the total score

## Key Insights

1. Since we can only move forward (i < j), we process positions in order
2. The score loss only happens when we switch between even and odd numbers
3. This suggests we need to track the best score we can achieve ending at each position, considering whether we're on an even or odd number
4. We can use dynamic programming where we track the maximum score achievable when ending at the current position with even or odd numbers

## Approach

We'll use dynamic programming with two states:
- `even_score`: maximum score achievable when the last visited position has an even number
- `odd_score`: maximum score achievable when the last visited position has an odd number

At each position i:
- If `nums[i]` is even, we can either:
  - Continue from an even position (no penalty)
  - Switch from odd position (pay penalty x)
- If `nums[i]` is odd, we can either:
  - Continue from an odd position (no penalty)
  - Switch from even position (pay penalty x)

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Initialize scores for even and odd positions
2. Start with position 0 (we always start here)
3. For each subsequent position, update the scores based on the current number's parity
4. The answer is the maximum of the two final scores

```python
class Solution:
    def maxScore(self, nums: List[int], x: int) -> int:
        # Initialize scores for even and odd numbers
        # We start at position 0, so we have nums[0] score initially
        even_score = nums[0] if nums[0] % 2 == 0 else 0
        odd_score = nums[0] if nums[0] % 2 == 1 else 0
        
        # Process each subsequent position
        for i in range(1, len(nums)):
            current = nums[i]
            
            if current % 2 == 0:  # current number is even
                # We can either continue from even position (no penalty)
                # or switch from odd position (penalty of x)
                even_score = current + max(even_score - x, 0)
                # Actually, we should just add current and compare with previous even_score
                # Let me correct this approach
                
        # Let me revise the approach - we should track the maximum score achievable
        # ending with even or odd numbers at each step
        
        # Reset and use correct DP approach
        even_score = nums[0] if nums[0] % 2 == 0 else 0
        odd_score = nums[0] if nums[0] % 2 == 1 else 0
        
        for i in range(1, len(nums)):
            current = nums[i]
            
            if current % 2 == 0:  # current number is even
                # Option 1: continue from even position (no penalty)
                # Option 2: switch from odd position (penalty x)
                new_even_score = current + max(even_score, odd_score - x)
                even_score = new_even_score
                # odd_score remains unchanged
            else:  # current number is odd
                # Option 1: continue from odd position (no penalty)
                # Option 2: switch from even position (penalty x)
                new_odd_score = current + max(odd_score, even_score - x)
                odd_score = new_odd_score
        
        return max(even_score, odd_score)
```

When we visit position i, we get `nums[i]` points, and we can either:
- Continue from a previous even position (if current is even) with no penalty
- Switch from previous odd position with penalty x

But the recurrence should be:
- `new_even_score = current + max(even_score, odd_score - x)`
- `new_odd_score = current + max(odd_score, even_score - x)`

This means we get the current value and add the best possible score from either continuing from the same parity or switching from the opposite parity with penalty.

Actually, I see the issue - when we update, we're not properly accounting for the score accumulation. Let me think again.

When we visit a position, we get the value of that position. We're building up the score from position 0 onward.

The DP states represent the maximum score achievable when ending at the current position with that parity.

So:
- If current is even, new_even_score = current + max(even_score, odd_score - x)
- This means we get current value and add the best score from continuing with even or switching from odd

- So: even_score=8, odd_score=13

- even_score = 2 + max(8, 13 - 5) = 2 + max(8, 8) = 2 + 8 = 10

```python
    def maxScore(self, nums: List[int], x: int) -> int:
        # Initialize scores for even and odd numbers
        # We start at position 0, so we have nums[0] score initially
        even_score = nums[0] if nums[0] % 2 == 0 else 0
        odd_score = nums[0] if nums[0] % 2 == 1 else 0
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Some number of people (this number is even) have stood in a circle. The people stand in the circle evenly. They are numbered clockwise starting from a person with the number $1$. Each person is looking through the circle's center at the opposite person.

A sample of a circle of $6$ persons. The orange arrows indicate who is looking at whom.

You don't know the exact number of people standing in the circle (but this number is even, no doubt). It is known that the person with the number $a$ is looking at the person with the number $b$ (and vice versa, of course). What is the number associated with a person being looked at by the person with the number $c$? If, for the specified $a$, $b$, and $c$, no such circle exists, output -1.


-----Input-----

- b = (a + n/2 - 1) % n + 1

- Person 1 looks at person 2 (opposite)
- Person 2 looks at person 1 (opposite)

So in this case, person with number 3 doesn't exist. This is why we have to check that the values are within 1 to n.

Actually, in the example: "2 3 1" gives output -1.

So if person 2 is looking at person 3, we need n ≥ 3. But with n = 2*|2-3| = 2, we have only people 1 and 2, so person 3 doesn't exist.

So we need to check that all the people a, b, c are within the range 1 to n.

In our solution, n = 2 * |a-b|, and we need:
- 1 ≤ a ≤ n
- 1 ≤ b ≤ n 
- 1 ≤ c ≤ n

Since a and b are given and positive, we have a ≥ 1, b ≥ 1.

We need n ≥ max(a,b).

So we need 2 * |a-b| ≥ max(a,b).

Let's check the examples:

Example 1: a=6, b=2, |a-b|=4, n=8, max(a,b)=6, 8 ≥ 6 ✓

Example 4: a=5, b=3, |a-b|=2, n=4, max(a,b)=5, 4 ≥ 5? No ✗ → output -1 ✓

Example 5: a=1, b=3, |a-b|=2, n=4, max(a,b)=3, 4 ≥ 3 ✓. c=2, which is ≤ 4. Person c=2 looks at (2 + 2 - 1) % 4 + 1 = 3 % 4 + 1 = 4. Expected output is 4 ✓

Example 6: a=2, b=5, |a-b|=3, n=6, max(a,b)=5, 6 ≥ 5 ✓. c=4, person 4 looks at (4 + 3 - 1) % 6 + 1 = 6 % 6 + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1. Expected output is 1 ✓

Example 7: a=4, b=3, |a-b|=1, n=2, max(a,b)=4, 2 ≥ 4? No ✗ → output -1 ✓

Perfect! So the algorithm is:

1. Compute diff = |a - b|
2. Compute n = 2 * diff
3. Check if n ≥ max(a, b) and c ≤ n (since c must be in the circle)
4. If not, return -1
5. Otherwise, compute d = (c + diff - 1) % n + 1
6. Return d

Wait, let me verify example 6: a=2, b=5, diff=3, n=6, max(a,b)=5, 6 ≥ 5 ✓, c=4 ≤ 6 ✓
d = (4 + 3 - 1) % 6 + 1 = 6 % 6 + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 ✓

Example 5: a=1, b=3, diff=2, n=4, max(a,b)=3, 4 ≥ 3 ✓, c=2 ≤ 4 ✓
d = (2 + 2 - 1) % 4 + 1 = 3 % 4 + 1 = 3 + 1 = 4 ✓

Example 3: a=2, b=4, diff=2, n=4, max(a,b)=4, 4 ≥ 4 ✓, c=10 > 4 → return -1 ✓

Example 2: a=2, b=3, diff=1, n=2, max(a,b)=3, 2 ≥ 3? No → return -1 ✓

All examples match!

## Final Algorithm

For each test case:
1. Read a, b, c
2. diff = |a - b|
3. n = 2 * diff
4. If n < max(a, b) or c > n: return -1
5. Otherwise: d = (c + diff - 1) % n + 1
6. Return d

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    a, b, c = map(int, input().split())
    
    diff = abs(a - b)
    n = 2 * diff
    
    # Check if the circle size is valid - a and b must be within [1, n]
    if n < max(a, b) or c > n:
        print(-1)
    else:
        # Person c looks at (c + diff - 1) % n + 1
        d = (c + diff - 1) % n + 1
        print(d)
```

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    a, b, c = map(int, input().split())
    
    diff = abs(a - b)
    n = 2 * diff
    
    # Check if the circle size is valid - a and b must be within [1, n]
    if n < max(a, b) or c > n:
        print(-1)
    else:
        # Person c looks at (c + diff - 1) % n + 1
        d = (c + diff - 1) % n + 1
        print(d)

- d divides 1, so d=1
- (1)/1 = 1, which is odd
- n = 2d = 2
- But person 3 is not in the circle of size 2 → invalid

So we must have b ≤ 2d and a ≤ 2d.

Actually, since the people are numbered consecutively from 1 to n, we need both a and b to be in [1, n].

So we need 1 ≤ a ≤ 2d and 1 ≤ b ≤ 2d.

In the example a=2, b=3: with d=1, n=2, but b=3 > 2 → invalid.

Are there other divisors? Divisors of 1 are only ±1, but only positive divisors matter since d > 0.

So no valid d → output -1.

This matches the expected output of -1.

Let me check another example: a=5, b=3, c=4
- b-a = -2
- Divisors of 2: 1, 2
- d=1: (-2)/1 = -2, which is even → invalid
- d=2: (-2)/2 = -1, which is odd → valid
- n = 4
- Check if a=5 and b=3 are in [1,4]? a=5 > 4 → invalid
- So output -1 → matches expected output.

Another example: a=1, b=3, c=2
- b-a = 2
- Divisors of 2: 1, 2
- d=1: 2/1 = 2, which is even → invalid
- d=2: 2/2 = 1, which is odd → valid
- n = 4
- a=1 ≤ 4, b=3 ≤ 4 → valid
- Person c=2 looks at (2 + 2) % 4 = 4, so answer is 4 → matches expected output.

Example: a=2, b=5, c=4
- b-a = 3
- Divisors of 3: 1, 3
- d=1: 3/1 = 3, odd → valid
- n = 2
- a=2 ≤ 2, b=5 > 2 → invalid
- d=3: 3/3 = 1, odd → valid  
- n = 6
- a=2 ≤ 6, b=5 ≤ 6 → valid
- Person c=4 looks at (4 + 3) % 6 = 7 % 6 = 1 → person 1? But we need (4 + d - 1) % n + 1 = (4 + 3 - 1) % 6 + 1 = 6 % 6 + 1 = 1
- Output 1 → matches expected output.

Example: a=4, b=3, c=2
- b-a = -1
- Divisors of 1: 1
- d=1: (-1)/1 = -1, which is odd → valid
- n = 2
- a=4 > 2 → invalid
- No other divisors → output -1 → matches expected output.

Wait, divisor of -1 is still 1 (positive divisor).
So only d=1, which gives n=2, but a=4 > 2 → invalid.

So the algorithm is:

For given a, b, c:
1. Compute diff = b - a
2. Find all positive divisors d of |diff| such that (diff/d) is odd
3. For each such divisor d:
   - n = 2 * d
   - If a ≤ n and b ≤ n, then person c looks at (c + d - 1) % n + 1
4. Return any valid answer (or -1 if none)

But we need to be careful about negative divisors. Since we're dealing with integers, we can consider the absolute value and then check the sign.

Actually, d must be a positive divisor of |diff|, and (diff/d) must be odd.

Note: if diff is negative, diff/d is negative, but negative odd numbers are odd. So we need to check if (diff/d) is odd (meaning the absolute value of diff/d is odd, and the sign doesn't matter for oddness).

In mathematics, oddness doesn't depend on sign. -1, -3, 1, 3 are all odd.

So we need |diff|/d to be odd.

Actually, (diff/d) is odd if and only if |diff|/d is odd (since the sign doesn't affect oddness).

So we can find all positive divisors of |diff| such that |diff|/d is odd.

Then for each such divisor d:
- n = 2 * d
- If a ≤ n and b ≤ n (both in valid range), then answer is (c + d - 1) % n + 1

But is there only one possible answer? The problem says "if there are multiple solutions, print any of them".

So we just need to find one valid solution.

However, there's a potential issue: what if diff = 0? But problem states a, b, c are distinct, so diff ≠ 0.

Also, what if |diff| is large? We need to find divisors efficiently.

Since |diff| can be up to 10^8, we need to be efficient. But the divisors of a number up to 10^8 might be many, but in practice we only need to check divisors up to sqrt(|diff|).

But worst case has many divisors (like perfect squares), but sqrt(10^8) = 10^4, which is acceptable.

Actually, worst-case number of divisors is about O(sqrt(n)), which for 10^8 is around 10^4, and we have up to 10^4 test cases → worst-case 10^8 operations, which might be borderline.

But let's check: average number of divisors is about log(n), so around 100 on average. So total operations would be about 10^4 * 100 = 10^6, which is fine.

So algorithm:

```python
def solve(a, b, c):
    diff = b - a
    abs_diff = abs(diff)
    
    # Find all divisors d of abs_diff such that abs_diff/d is odd
    valid_d = []
    
    # Check divisors up to sqrt(abs_diff)
    i = 1
    while i * i <= abs_diff:
        if abs_diff % i == 0:
            # i is a divisor
            d1 = i
            d2 = abs_diff // i
            
            # Check if abs_diff/d1 is odd
            if abs_diff // d1 % 2 == 1:
                if a <= 2 * d1 and b <= 2 * d1:
                    valid_d.append(d1)
            
            # Check if abs_diff/d2 is odd (for d2, if d2 != d1)
            if d1 != d2:
                if abs_diff // d2 % 2 == 1:
                    if a <= 2 * d2 and b <= 2 * d2:
                        valid_d.append(d2)
        i += 1
    
    # If we found valid solutions, return any one
    if valid_d:
        d = valid_d[0]
        n = 2 * d
        # Person c looks at (c + d - 1) % n + 1
        result = (c + d - 1) % n + 1
        return result
    else:
        return -1
```

Edge case: what if a=3, b=1, c=2?
- diff = -2, abs_diff = 2
- Divisors: 1, 2

- result = (2 + 2 - 1) % 4 + 1 = 3 % 4 + 1 = 4

In circle of 4: person 1 looks at 3 (1+2), person 3 looks at 1 (3+2=5%4+1=1), person 2 looks at 4.
So person 2 looks at 4 → correct.

```python
def solve(a, b, c):
    diff = b - a
    abs_diff = abs(diff)
    
    valid_d = []
    
    i = 1
    while i * i <= abs_diff:
        if abs_diff % i == 0:
            # i is a divisor
            d1 = i
            d2 = abs_diff // i
            
            # Check if abs_diff/d1 is odd
            if abs_diff // d1 % 2 == 1:
                if a <= 2 * d1 and b <= 2 * d1:
                    valid_d.append(d1)
            
            # Check if abs_diff/d2 is odd (for d2, if d2 != d1)
            if d1 != d2 and abs_diff // d2 % 2 == 1:
                if a <= 2 * d2 and b <= 2 * d2:
                    valid_d.append(d2)
        i += 1
    
    if valid_d:
        d = valid_d[0]
        n = 2 * d
        result = (c + d - 1) % n + 1
        return result
    else:
        return -1

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    a, b, c = map(int, input().split())
    print(solve(a, b, c))
```

def solve(a, b, c):
    diff = b - a
    abs_diff = abs(diff)
    
    valid_d = []
    
    i = 1
    while i * i <= abs_diff:
        if abs_diff % i == 0:
            # i is a divisor
            d1 = i
            d2 = abs_diff // i
            
            # Check if abs_diff/d1 is odd
            if abs_diff // d1 % 2 == 1:
                if a <= 2 * d1 and b <= 2 * d1:
                    valid_d.append(d1)
            
            # Check if abs_diff/d2 is odd (for d2, if d2 != d1)
            if d1 != d2 and abs_diff // d2 % 2 == 1:
                if a <= 2 * d2 and b <= 2 * d2:
                    valid_d.append(d2)
        i += 1
    
    if valid_d:
        d = valid_d[0]
        n = 2 * d
        result = (c + d - 1) % n + 1
        return result
    else:
        return -1

t = int(input())

    print(solve(a, b, c))

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Some number of people (this number is even) have stood in a circle. The people stand in the circle evenly. They are numbered clockwise starting from a person with the number $1$. Each person is looking through the circle's center at the opposite person.

A sample of a circle of $6$ persons. The orange arrows indicate who is looking at whom.

You don't know the exact number of people standing in the circle (but this number is even, no doubt). It is known that the person with the number $a$ is looking at the person with the number $b$ (and vice versa, of course). What is the number associated with a person being looked at by the person with the number $c$? If, for the specified $a$, $b$, and $c$, no such circle exists, output -1.


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 10^4$) — the number of test cases. Then $t$ test cases follow.

Each test case consists of one line containing three distinct integers $a$, $b$, $c$ ($1 \le a,b,c \le 10^8$).


-----Output-----

For each test case output in a separate line a single integer $d$ — the number of the person being looked at by the person with the number $c$ in a circle such that the person with the number $a$ is looking at the person with the number $b$. If there are multiple solutions, print any of them. Output $-1$ if there's no circle meeting the given conditions.


-----Examples-----

Input
7
6 2 4
2 3 1
2 4 10
5 3 4
1 3 2
2 5 4
4 3 2
Output
8
-1
-1
-1
4
1
-1


-----Note-----

In the first test case, there's a desired circle of $8$ people. The person with the number $6$ will look at the person with the number $2$ and the person with the number $8$ will look at the person with the number $4$.

In the second test case, there's no circle meeting the conditions. If the person with the number $2$ is looking at the person with the number $3$, the circle consists of $2$ people because these persons are neighbors. But, in this case, they must have the numbers $1$ and $2$, but it doesn't meet the problem's conditions.

In the third test case, the only circle with the persons with the numbers $2$ and $4$ looking at each other consists of $4$ people. Therefore, the person with the number $10$ doesn't occur in the circle.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

=> (a + n/2 - 1) % n = b - 1
=> a + n/2 - 1 ≡ b - 1 (mod n)
=> a + n/2 ≡ b (mod n)
=> n/2 ≡ b - a (mod n)

1. n/2 ≡ b - a (mod n)
2. 1 ≤ a,b ≤ n

From condition 1: n/2 ≡ b - a (mod n), which implies b - a ≡ n/2 (mod n)

This means b - a - n/2 is divisible by n.

So b - a - n/2 = k*n for some integer k.

So b - a = n/2 + k*n = (2k + 1) * (n/2)

Therefore, n/2 divides (b - a), and (b - a) / (n/2) is odd.

Let k = n/2, then b - a = (2m + 1) * k for some integer m.

So k divides (b - a), and (b - a) / k is odd.

Now we also need 1 ≤ a,b ≤ n = 2k.

So we need a ≤ 2k and b ≤ 2k, and a ≥ 1, b ≥ 1.

For each odd divisor of d, we get a candidate k = |d| / (2m+1) if d > 0, or k = |d| / (2m+1) if d < 0 (with appropriate sign).

If d < 0, we need k = d / o = negative number, which is invalid since k > 0.

Wait, let's see: d = (2m+1)*k = o*k.

Therefore, d must be positive.

So we need b > a.

In the example "2 3 1": a=2, b=3, so b > a, d = 1.

Therefore, answer is -1.

Another example: "6 2 4" -> a=6, b=2, c=4

a=6, b=2, so b < a, d = b-a = -4

We need b > a for d > 0, so we have a problem.

Contradiction.

What's wrong?

The derivation was that b - a ≡ n/2 (mod n)

The right side is always odd multiple of n/2.

Person 4 looks at (4 + 4 - 1) % 8 + 1 = 7 % 8 + 1 = 7 + 1 = 8.

So answer is 8.

Perfect.

So general algorithm:

For given a,b,c:
1. If a == b or b == c or a == c, invalid (but problem states distinct integers)
2. Compute d = b - a
3. Find all odd positive integers t such that t divides |d| and t divides |d| (t is odd divisor of |d|)
4. For each such t, set k = |d| / t
5. Then d = (2m + 1) * k = ± t * k
6. We need to solve: b - a = sign * t * k for sign = ±1

Actually, from b - a = (2m + 1) * (n/2) = (2m + 1) * k

So b - a = (2m + 1) * k

This means (b - a) / k must be an odd integer.

The key is that (b - a) / k must be an odd integer.

(b - a) / k = (b - a) * t / |d|

Since |d| = |b - a|, this is:
(b - a) * t / |b - a| = sign(t) where sign = (b-a)/|b-a| = ±1

So (b - a) / k = sign * t

This is an integer that is odd only if t is odd (which it is).

So for every odd divisor t of |d|, we get a valid k = |d| / t, and (b - a) / k = ±t, which is odd.

Therefore, for each odd divisor t of |b-a|, we get:
- k = |b-a| / t
- n = 2k
- We need to verify that 1 ≤ a, b ≤ n (since the circle has n people)

Then for such a valid n, person c looks at:
x = (c + k - 1) % n + 1

But we need to ensure that in this configuration, person a looks at person b.

Since we derived k from the condition, this should hold.

So algorithm:

1. For each test case with a,b,c:
2. Let d = b - a
3. If d == 0: invalid (but inputs are distinct)
4. Let abs_d = |d|
5. Find all odd divisors of abs_d
6. For each odd divisor t of abs_d:
   - k = abs_d / t
   - n = 2 * k
   - If a < 1 or a > n or b < 1 or b > n: skip
   - Otherwise, compute x = (c + k - 1) % n + 1
   - We can return any such x (since the problem says "if there are multiple solutions, print any of them")
7. If no valid n found, return -1

## Implementation

```python
def get_odd_divisors(n):
    divisors = []
    i = 1
    while i * i <= n:
        if n % i == 0:
            if i % 2 == 1:
                divisors.append(i)
            if i != n // i and (n // i) % 2 == 1:
                divisors.append(n // i)
        i += 1
    return divisors

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    a, b, c = map(int, input().split())
    
    d = b - a
    abs_d = abs(d)
    
    if abs_d == 0:
        print(-1)
        continue
        
    odd_divisors = get_odd_divisors(abs_d)
    
    found = False
    result = -1
    
    for t_val in odd_divisors:
        k = abs_d // t_val
        n = 2 * k
        
        # Check if a and b are within [1, n]
        if a < 1 or a > n or b < 1 or b > n:
            continue
            
        # Calculate who c looks at
        # Person c looks at (c + k - 1) % n + 1
        x = (c + k - 1) % n + 1
        result = x
        found = True
        break
    
    print(result if found else -1)
```

    divisors = []

            if i != n // i and (n // i) % 2 == 1:

        i += 1
    return divisors

    abs_d = abs(d)
    
    if abs_d == 0:
        print(-1)
        continue
        
    odd_divisors = get_odd_divisors(abs_d)
    
    found = False
    result = -1
    
    for t_val in odd_divisors:

        n = 2 * k

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Some number of people (this number is even) have stood in a circle. The people stand in the circle evenly. They are numbered clockwise starting from a person with the number $1$. Each person is looking through the circle's center at the opposite person.

A sample of a circle of $6$ persons. The orange arrows indicate who is looking at whom.

You don't know the exact number of people standing in the circle (but this number is even, no doubt). It is known that the person with the number $a$ is looking at the person with the number $b$ (and vice versa, of course). What is the number associated with a person being looked at by the person with the number $c$? If, for the specified $a$, $b$, and $c$, no such circle exists, output -1.


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 10^4$) — the number of test cases. Then $t$ test cases follow.

Each test case consists of one line containing three distinct integers $a$, $b$, $c$ ($1 \le a,b,c \le 10^8$).


-----Output-----

For each test case output in a separate line a single integer $d$ — the number of the person being looked at by the person with the number $c$ in a circle such that the person with the number $a$ is looking at the person with the number $b$. If there are multiple solutions, print any of them. Output $-1$ if there's no circle meeting the given conditions.


-----Examples-----

Input
7
6 2 4
2 3 1
2 4 10
5 3 4
1 3 2
2 5 4
4 3 2
Output
8
-1
-1
-1
4
1
-1


-----Note-----

In the first test case, there's a desired circle of $8$ people. The person with the number $6$ will look at the person with the number $2$ and the person with the number $8$ will look at the person with the number $4$.

In the second test case, there's no circle meeting the conditions. If the person with the number $2$ is looking at the person with the number $3$, the circle consists of $2$ people because these persons are neighbors. But, in this case, they must have the numbers $1$ and $2$, but it doesn't meet the problem's conditions.

In the third test case, the only circle with the persons with the numbers $2$ and $4$ looking at each other consists of $4$ people. Therefore, the person with the number $10$ doesn't occur in the circle.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs).
