n(1 - 2k) = 2

So n = 2 / (1 - 2k)

For k=0: n = 2/1 = 2
For k=1: n = 2/(1-2) = 2/(-1) = -2 (invalid)
For k=-1: n = 2/(1+2) = 2/3 (not integer)

So only possible n=2. But in n=2, person 1 looks at person 2, person 2 looks at person 1. So person 2 cannot look at person 3. Contradiction.

So no solution exists. Output -1. Which matches the expected output.

The issue is that in our derivation, when we set up the equation n/2 ≡ diff (mod n), we assumed that the position difference is exactly n/2. But this only works if the positions are valid.

Let me go back to the fundamental equation:

For person a to look at person b, we have:
( a + n/2 - 1 ) % n + 1 = b
So (a + n/2 - 1) % n = b - 1
So a + n/2 - 1 ≡ b - 1 (mod n)
So a + n/2 ≡ b (mod n)
So n/2 ≡ b - a (mod n)

This is correct. So we need to solve n/2 ≡ (b - a) (mod n) for even positive n.

From n/2 ≡ (b - a) (mod n), we get:
n/2 - (b - a) = k*n for some integer k
n/2 - b + a = k*n
n/2 - k*n = b - a
n(1/2 - k) = b - a
n(1 - 2k)/2 = b - a
n(1 - 2k) = 2(b - a)

So n = 2(b - a) / (1 - 2k)

For integer k, n must be positive even.

So for each integer k, compute n = 2(b-a)/(1-2k). We need this to be a positive even integer.

1-2k is always odd, and we need it to divide 2(b-a).

So the valid k values are those where (1-2k) divides 2(b-a) and n > 0 and even.

Since n must be even, 2(b-a)/(1-2k) must be even, so (b-a)/(1-2k) must be integer.

So (1-2k) must divide (b-a).

Actually, let's simplify: n = 2(b-a)/(1-2k)

n > 0 means if (b-a) > 0, then (1-2k) > 0, so 1-2k > 0, k < 1/2
If (b-a) < 0, then (1-2k) < 0, so 1-2k < 0, k > 1/2

And n must be even, so 2(b-a)/(1-2k) must be even.

Let d = 1 - 2k, which is an odd integer (since 2k is even).

Then n = 2(b-a)/d, and we need:
1. d divides 2(b-a)
2. n > 0
3. n is even

Since d is odd, for n to be even, 2(b-a)/d must be even, so (b-a)/d must be integer.

Therefore, d must divide (b-a).

So d is an odd divisor of (b-a) (or - (b-a), since divisors can be negative).

We need d | (b-a) and d ≠ 0, and n = 2(b-a)/d > 0 and even.

For each odd divisor d of (b-a) (positive and negative), we get:
- If d > 0 and (b-a) > 0: n = 2(b-a)/d > 0, and we need it to be even
- If d < 0 and (b-a) < 0: n = 2(b-a)/d > 0, and we need it to be even  
- If d > 0 and (b-a) < 0: n < 0 (invalid)
- If d < 0 and (b-a) > 0: n < 0 (invalid)

So only when (b-a) and d have the same sign.

For each odd divisor d of (b-a) with d and (b-a) having the same sign, compute n = 2(b-a)/d.

Then check if n > 0 and is even.

If such n exists, then person c looks at person (c + n/2 - 1) % n + 1.

If no such n exists, output -1.

Let me verify with example 2: a=2, b=3, c=1
b-a = 1
Odd divisors of 1: ±1
d=1 > 0, b-a=1 > 0: n = 2*1/1 = 2, which is even and positive ✓
d=-1 < 0, b-a=1 > 0: same sign? no, negative vs positive, invalid

So n=2 is valid.
Person c=1 looks at (1 + 2/2 - 1) % 2 + 1 = (1 + 1 - 1) % 2 + 1 = 1 % 2 + 1 = 1 + 1 = 2.

But we need to check if person a=2 looks at person b=3. In n=2, people are 1,2. Person 2 looks at person 1, not person 3. Contradiction.

What's wrong?

Go back to the equation:
We derived that if person a looks at person b, then n/2 ≡ b - a (mod n)

For a=2, b=3, n=2: n/2 = 1, b-a=1, so 1 ≡ 1 (mod 2)? Yes, 1 ≡ 1 (mod 2).

But in a circle of 2 people: person 1 looks at person 2, person 2 looks at person 1.

So person 2 cannot look at person 3. The issue is that person 3 doesn't exist in a circle of 2 people.

Ah! I see. We also need that 1 ≤ b ≤ n.

So in our solution, we must have 1 ≤ b ≤ n.

In the example, b = 3, n = 2, so 3 > 2, which is invalid.

So we need to add the constraint that b must be between 1 and n inclusive.

Similarly, c must be between 1 and n inclusive.

So algorithm:

For each test case (a,b,c):
1. Let diff = b - a
2. Find all odd divisors d of diff (positive and negative)
3. For each odd divisor d where d and diff have the same sign:
   - Compute n = 2 * diff / d
   - If n is positive even integer and 1 ≤ b ≤ n and 1 ≤ c ≤ n:
     - Then person c looks at person (c + n//2 - 1) % n + 1
     - Return that person
4. If no valid n found, return -1

Note: Since diff can be very large (up to 10^8), we need to find odd divisors efficiently.

We can do this by iterating through odd numbers up to sqrt(|diff|).

But |diff| can be up to 10^8, so sqrt is about 10^4, which is acceptable since t ≤ 10^4, but worst case 10^4 * 10^4 = 10^8, which might be borderline in Python.

But we only need divisors, and each number has at most O(sqrt(n)) divisors, so total operations would be O(t * sqrt(|diff|)).

In worst case, |diff| = 10^8, sqrt(10^8) ≈ 10^4, so 10^4 * 10^4 = 10^8 operations, which might be too slow in Python.

We need to optimize.

Actually, we only need to find odd divisors. So we can factor out all factors of 2 from diff, and work with the odd part.

Let odd_part = diff, and repeatedly divide by 2 until odd.

Then find odd divisors of odd_part.

For each odd divisor d of odd_part, we have the corresponding d (positive and negative).

But note: if diff = 0, then we have a problem. But a, b, c are distinct, so b ≠ a, so diff ≠ 0.

So we're safe.

Algorithm for finding odd divisors:
1. Let x = abs(diff)
2. Find all odd divisors of x by iterating i from 1 to sqrt(x), only for odd i
3. For each odd i that divides x, add i and x//i to divisors (if i != x//i)

Then for each divisor d of diff (positive and negative), check conditions.

But since we only care about d with same sign as diff, we can:
- For positive divisors of |diff|, if diff > 0, use positive divisors
- For negative divisors of |diff|, if diff < 0, use negative divisors

Actually, the negative divisors of diff are just -divisors of |diff|.

So we can:
1. Let x = abs(diff)
2. Find all odd divisors of x
3. For each divisor d in divisors:
   - If diff > 0 and d > 0: consider d
   - If diff < 0 and d < 0: consider d
   - If diff > 0 and d < 0: skip
   - If diff < 0 and d > 0: skip

Then for each such d, compute n = 2 * diff / d

Check if n is positive even integer and 1 ≤ b ≤ n and 1 ≤ c ≤ n.

If yes, compute answer = (c + n//2 - 1) % n + 1 and return it.

```python
import math

def get_odd_divisors(x):
    divisors = set()
    i = 1
    while i * i <= x:
        if x % i == 0:
            if i % 2 == 1:
                divisors.add(i)
                divisors.add(x // i)
        i += 1
    return divisors

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    a, b, c = map(int, input().split())
    diff = b - a
    
    # If diff is 0, but a,b distinct, so diff != 0
    x = abs(diff)
    
    # Get all odd divisors of x
    odd_divisors = get_odd_divisors(x)
    
    found = False
    ans = -1
    
    for d in odd_divisors:
        # Only consider d with same sign as diff
        if diff > 0:
            if d > 0:
                n = 2 * diff // d
                if n > 0 and n % 2 == 0:
                    # Check if all people a,b,c are in range [1, n]
                    if 1 <= a <= n and 1 <= b <= n and 1 <= c <= n:
                        # Person c looks at person (c + n//2 - 1) % n + 1
                        opposite = (c + n // 2 - 1) % n + 1
                        ans = opposite
                        found = True
                        break
        else:  # diff < 0
            if d < 0:
                n = 2 * diff // d
                if n > 0 and n % 2 == 0:
                    if 1 <= a <= n and 1 <= b <= n and 1 <= c <= n:
                        opposite = (c + n // 2 - 1) % n + 1
                        ans = opposite
                        found = True
                        break
    
    print(ans)
```

Final code:

```python
import math

def get_odd_divisors(x):
    divisors = set()
    i = 1
    while i * i <= x:
        if x % i == 0:
            if i % 2 == 1:
                divisors.add(i)
                divisors.add(x // i)
        i += 2
    return divisors

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    a, b, c = map(int, input().split())
    diff = b - a
    x = abs(diff)
    
    if x == 0:
        # But a,b distinct, so x != 0
        pass
        
    odd_divisors = get_odd_divisors(x)
    found = False
    ans = -1
    
    for d in odd_divisors:
        # Consider positive and negative divisors
        # For each positive divisor, we have d and -d
        # But we only consider d with sign matching diff
        if diff > 0:
            # Only consider positive d
            if d > 0:
                n = 2 * diff // d
                if n > 0 and n % 2 == 0:
                    if 1 <= a <= n and 1 <= b <= n and 1 <= c <= n:
                        opposite = (c + n // 2 - 1) % n + 1
                        ans = opposite
                        found = True
                        break
        else:  # diff < 0
            # Only consider negative d
            if d < 0:
                n = 2 * diff // d
                if n > 0 and n % 2 == 0:
                    if 1 <= a <= n and 1 <= b <= n and 1 <= c <= n:
                        opposite = (c + n // 2 - 1) % n + 1
                        ans = opposite
                        found = True
                        break
    
    print(ans)
```

Wait, but in the loop, d is from the set of positive odd divisors. We are only considering positive d for diff>0 and negative d for diff<0.

But when we iterate over odd_divisors, we have only positive divisors. So we need to consider both signs.

So we should do:

```python
for d in odd_divisors:
    if diff > 0:
        if d > 0:
            # process d
        if d < 0:
            # process d
    else:
        if d < 0:
            # process d
        if d > 0:
            # process d
```

But in our function, we only return positive divisors.

Actually, we should generate negative divisors.

So modify get_odd_divisors to return both positive and negative divisors?

But we don't need to, we can just consider both signs.

So in the loop, for each positive divisor, we can consider both d and -d.

But we only want the sign to match diff.

So:

```python
for d in odd_divisors:  # d is positive
    if diff > 0:
        # try d (positive) and -d (negative)
        # but sign: d>0, -d<0
        # so only d>0 matches
        if d > 0:
            n = 2 * diff // d
            if n > 0 and n % 2 == 0 and 1 <= a <= n and 1 <= b <= n and 1 <= c <= n:
                ans = (c + n//2 - 1) % n + 1
                found = True
                break
        if d < 0:  # d is positive, so this never happens
            pass
    else:  # diff < 0
        if d < 0:
            n = 2 * diff // d
            if n > 0 and n % 2 == 0 and 1 <= a <= n and 1 <= b <= n and 1 <= c <= n:
                ans = (c + n//2 - 1) % n + 1
                found = True
                break
        if d > 0:
            pass
```

This is messy.

Instead, we can create a list of candidate divisors: for each positive divisor d of x, add d and -d.

Then iterate over all of them.

```python
def get_odd_divisors(x):
    divisors = set()
    i = 1
    while i * i <= x:
        if x % i == 0:
            if i % 2 == 1:
                divisors.add(i)
                divisors.add(x // i)
        i += 2
    # Now divisors has only positive odd divisors
    # We need to return both positive and negative
    # But we will iterate and consider signs
    return divisors

# Then in the loop:
candidates = []
for d in divisors:
    candidates.append(d)
    candidates.append(-d)

for d in candidates:
    if diff > 0 and d > 0:
        n = 2 * diff // d
        if n > 0 and n % 2 == 0 and 1 <= a <= n and 1 <= b <= n and 1 <= c <= n:
            ans = (c + n//2 - 1) % n + 1
            found = True
            break
    elif diff < 0 and d < 0:
        n = 2 * diff // d
        if n > 0 and n % 2 == 0 and 1 <= a <= n and 1 <= b <= n and 1 <= c <= n:
            ans = (c + n//2 - 1) % n + 1
            found = True
            break
```

But is this correct? For diff > 0, we only consider positive d, for diff < 0, only negative d.

Yes.

But we can avoid creating a list and just iterate over the positive divisors.

```python
for d in divisors:  # d is positive
    if diff > 0:
        n = 2 * diff // d
        if n > 0 and n % 2 == 0 and 1 <= a <= n and 1 <= b <= n and 1 <= c <= n:
            ans = (c + n//2 - 1) % n + 1
            found = True
            break
    else:  # diff < 0
        n = 2 * diff // (-d)
        if n > 0 and n % 2 == 0 and 1 <= a <= n and 1 <= b <= n and 1 <= c <= n:
            ans = (c + n//2 - 1) % n + 1
            found = True
            break
```

Wait, for diff < 0, we want d < 0, so we use -d as positive divisor.

So n = 2 * diff // (-d)

Yes.

And we only need to iterate over positive odd divisors.

This is cleaner.

So final code:

```python
def get_odd_divisors(x):
    divisors = set()
    i = 1
    while i * i <= x:
        if x % i == 0:
            if i % 2 == 1:
                divisors.add(i)
                divisors.add(x // i)
        i += 2
    return divisors

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    a, b, c = map(int, input().split())
    diff = b - a
    x = abs(diff)
    
    odd_divisors = get_odd_divisors(x)
    found = False
    ans = -1
    
    for d in odd_divisors:
        if diff > 0:
            n = 2 * diff // d
            if n > 0 and n % 2 == 0:
                if 1 <= a <= n and 1 <= b <= n and 1 <= c <= n:
                    opposite = (c + n // 2 - 1) % n + 1
                    ans = opposite
                    found = True
                    break
        else:  # diff < 0
            n = 2 * diff // (-d)
            if n > 0 and n % 2 == 0:
                if 1 <= a <= n and 1 <= b <= n and 1 <= c <= n:
                    opposite = (c + n // 2 - 1) % n + 1
                    ans = opposite
                    found = True
                    break
    
    print(ans)
```

Let me test with example 1: diff = -4, d=1 (positive)
n = 2*(-4) // (-1) = 8 ✓

In code: diff < 0, so n = 2*diff // (-d) = 2*(-4) // (-1) = 8 ✓

Example 5: diff=2, d=1
n = 2*2 // 1 = 4 ✓

All good.

Edge: if no divisor found, ans remains -1.

We're done.

Note: the division in Python for negative numbers: 2*diff // d, when diff<0 and d<0, should be positive.

In Python, // is floor division, but since both are negative, it should be positive.

For example: -4 // -1 = 4, which is correct.

In our case, when diff < 0, we use -d, which is positive, so 2*diff is negative, -d is positive, so negative // positive = negative.

Wait! Mistake!

In example: diff = -4, d = 1 (positive)
We want n = 2 * (-4) // (-1) = 8

But in code: diff < 0, so n = 2 * diff // (-d) = 2*(-4) // (-1) = 8

2*(-4) = -8, -d = -1 (since d=1), so n = -8 // -1 = 8 ✓

Yes, because negative // negative = positive.

And in Python, -8 // -1 = 8.

So it's correct.

Therefore, final code:

def get_odd_divisors(x):
    divisors = set()
    i = 1
    while i * i <= x:
        if x % i == 0:
            if i % 2 == 1:
                divisors.add(i)
                divisors.add(x // i)
        i += 2
    return divisors

    x = abs(diff)

    ans = -1

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Some number of people (this number is even) have stood in a circle. The people stand in the circle evenly. They are numbered clockwise starting from a person with the number $1$. Each person is looking through the circle's center at the opposite person.

A sample of a circle of $6$ persons. The orange arrows indicate who is looking at whom.

You don't know the exact number of people standing in the circle (but this number is even, no doubt). It is known that the person with the number $a$ is looking at the person with the number $b$ (and vice versa, of course). What is the number associated with a person being looked at by the person with the number $c$? If, for the specified $a$, $b$, and $c$, no such circle exists, output -1.


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 10^4$) — the number of test cases. Then $t$ test cases follow.

Each test case consists of one line containing three distinct integers $a$, $b$, $c$ ($1 \le a,b,c \le 10^8$).


-----Output-----

For each test case output in a separate line a single integer $d$ — the number of the person being looked at by the person with the number $c$ in a circle such that the person with the number $a$ is looking at the person with the number $b$. If there are multiple solutions, print any of them. Output $-1$ if there's no circle meeting the given conditions.


-----Examples-----

Input

2 3 1
2 4 10
5 3 4
1 3 2
2 5 4
4 3 2
Output
8
-1
-1
-1
4
1
-1

In the second test case, there's no circle meeting the conditions. If the person with the number $2$ is looking at the person with the number $3$, the circle consists of $2$ people because these persons are neighbors. In the third test case, the only circle with the persons with the numbers $2$ and $4$ looking at each other consists of $4$ people. Therefore, the person with the number $10$ doesn't occur in the circle.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

## Problem Understanding

1. People stand in a circle with even number of people
2. Each person looks at the person directly opposite them
3. We're given that person `a` looks at person `b`, and we need to find who person `c` looks at
4. The circle has even number of people, so opposite person is exactly half the circle away

## Key Insights

1. In a circle of `n` people (even), person `i` looks at person `i + n/2` (mod n)
2. If person `a` looks at person `b`, then `b = a + n/2` (mod n)
3. This means `b - a` must be exactly `n/2` (mod n)

## Mathematical Analysis

If person `a` looks at person `b`, then:
- `b = a + n/2` (mod n)
- So `b - a = n/2` (mod n)

This means `(b - a)` must be exactly `n/2` or `(b - a) ≡ n/2 (mod n)`.

Since `b - a` is the distance from a to b, and we're working mod n, we have:
- `n/2 = (b - a) mod n`

But since `n/2` is half of n, and `b - a` is less than n, we have:
- `n/2 = |b - a|` or `n/2 = b - a` (if b > a) or `n/2 = a - b` (if a > b)

Actually, since we're in a circle, we need to consider the circular distance. But notice that in a circle of n people, if person a looks at person b, then the distance between them is exactly n/2.

So the distance between a and b is n/2, which means:
- `|b - a| = n/2` OR `|b - a| = n - n/2 = n/2` (same thing)

Wait, let me reconsider. In a circle of n people, the distance between person a and person b is min(|b-a|, n-|b-a|). But since they're opposite each other, the distance must be exactly n/2.

So we have: `|b - a| = n/2`

Therefore: `n = 2 * |b - a|`

This means the total number of people in the circle must be exactly `2 * |b - a|`.

- We need to ensure that the person being looked at has a valid number

1. Calculate d = |b - a|
2. Calculate n = 2 * d
3. Check if 1 ≤ a ≤ n and 1 ≤ b ≤ n and 1 ≤ c ≤ n
4. If any of these conditions fail, return -1
5. Otherwise, person c looks at person (c + n//2 - 1) % n + 1

- d = |5-2| = 3

## Final Algorithm

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    a, b, c = map(int, input().split())
    
    d = abs(b - a)
    n = 2 * d
    
    # Check if all people a, b, c are within the circle of n people
    if a < 1 or a > n or b < 1 or b > n or c < 1 or c > n:
        print(-1)
    else:
        # Person c looks at person (c + n//2 - 1) % n + 1
        opposite = (c + n // 2 - 1) % n + 1
        print(opposite)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. People stand in a circle with even number of people, numbered clockwise from 1
2. Each person looks at the person directly opposite them
3. We know that person `a` is looking at person `b`
4. We need to find who person `c` is looking at
5. If no such circle exists, return -1

## Key Insights

In a circle with `n` people (where `n` is even), the person at position `i` looks at the person at position `(i + n/2) % n`.

Since people are numbered from 1 to n, we need to use modular arithmetic carefully.

If person `a` is looking at person `b` in a circle of `n` people, then:
- The distance between `a` and `b` should be exactly `n/2` positions
- So we have: `(b - a) % n = n/2` or `(a - b) % n = n/2`

This means: `b ≡ a + n/2 (mod n)` or `b ≡ a - n/2 (mod n)`

So: `b - a ≡ ±n/2 (mod n)`

Rearranging: `n/2 ≡ (b - a) % n` or `n/2 ≡ (a - b) % n`

Therefore: `n/2 = |b - a| / gcd(|b - a|, n)`... let me think differently.

Actually, let's think about it this way:
- If person `a` is looking at person `b`, then the distance between them is exactly `n/2`
- The positions are cyclic, so `|b - a|` should be exactly `n/2` or `n - |b - a|` should be exactly `n/2`
- This means `|b - a| ≡ n/2 (mod n)`

So `n/2 = |b - a|` or `n/2 = n - |b - a|`

Wait, that's not quite right either. Let me reconsider.

In a circle of n people, the distance between person i and person j is min(|i-j|, n-|i-j|). For opposite positions, the distance is exactly n/2.

So we have: min(|a-b|, n-|a-b|) = n/2

This means |a-b| = n/2 (since n/2 is exactly half of n, and n/2 ≥ n/2)

So |a-b| = n/2, which means n = 2 * |a-b|

Therefore, n must be exactly 2 * |a-b|.

Wait, is that always true? Let's check with the example:
- a=6, b=2, so |6-2| = 4, so n = 8
- In a circle of 8 people, person 6 looks at person 2? 
  - Position of 6: 6, opposite is 6 + 4 = 10, which is 10 mod 8 = 2. Yes!

So the circle size n must be exactly 2 * |a-b|.

But what if a=2, b=3? |2-3| = 1, so n = 2, so only people 1 and 2.
- Person 1 looks at person 2, person 2 looks at person 1.
- But if person 2 looks at person 3, and 3 doesn't exist in a circle of size 2, this is impossible.

So for a valid circle, we need:
1. The circle size n = 2 * |a-b| (this gives us the total number of people)
2. All people involved must have numbers between 1 and n
3. Person c must be in the circle (have number between 1 and n)
4. In a circle of n people, person x looks at person (x + n/2) % n, but we have to be careful with modular arithmetic

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    a, b, c = map(int, input().split())
    
    d = abs(a - b)
    n = 2 * d
    
    # Check if a and b are within the circle bounds
    if a > n or b > n:
        print(-1)
    # Check if c is within the circle bounds
    elif c > n:
        print(-1)
    else:
        k = d
        # Person c looks at (c + k - 1) % n + 1
        result = (c + k - 1) % n + 1
        print(result)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Some number of people (this number is even) have stood in a circle. The people stand in the circle evenly. They are numbered clockwise starting from a person with the number $1$. Each person is looking through the circle's center at the opposite person.

A sample of a circle of $6$ persons. The orange arrows indicate who is looking at whom.

You don't know the exact number of people standing in the circle (but this number is even, no doubt). It is known that the person with the number $a$ is looking at the person with the number $b$ (and vice versa, of course). What is the number associated with a person being looked at by the person with the number $c$? If, for the specified $a$, $b$, and $c$, no such circle exists, output -1.


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 10^4$) — the number of test cases. Then $t$ test cases follow.

Each test case consists of one line containing three distinct integers $a$, $b$, $c$ ($1 \le a,b,c \le 10^8$).


-----Output-----

For each test case output in a separate line a single integer $d$ — the number of the person being looked at by the person with the number $c$ in a circle such that the person with the number $a$ is looking at the person with the number $b$. If there are multiple solutions, print any of them. Output $-1$ if there's no circle meeting the given conditions.


-----Examples-----

Input
7
6 2 4
2 3 1
2 4 10
5 3 4
1 3 2
2 5 4
4 3 2
Output
8
-1
-1
-1
4
1
-1


-----Note-----

In the first test case, there's a desired circle of $8$ people. The person with the number $6$ will look at the person with the number $2$ and the person with the number $8$ will look at the person with the number $4$.

In the second test case, there's no circle meeting the conditions. If the person with the number $2$ is looking at the person with the number $3$, the circle consists of $2$ people because these persons are neighbors. But, in this case, they must have the numbers $1$ and $2$, but it doesn't meet the problem's conditions.

In the third test case, the only circle with the persons with the numbers $2$ and $4$ looking at each other consists of $4$ people. Therefore, the person with the number $10$ doesn't occur in the circle.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). - target = (4 + 6//2 - 1) % 6 + 1 = (4+3-1)%6 +1 = 6%6 +1 = 0+1 = 1
- So output 1 ✓

Example 7: a=4, b=3, c=2
- diff = 1
- d=1: n=2
- max(4,3,2)=4 > 2 ✗
- output -1 ✓

Perfect! This matches all examples.

But wait, in example 6, a=2, b=5, c=4. With n=6:
- Person 2 should look at person 2 + 3 = 5 mod 6 = 5. Correct.
- Person 4 should look at person 4 + 3 = 7 mod 6 = 1. Correct.
- So answer is 1.

In the output, it's given as 1. But in the example output it says "1", so yes.

We need to implement this efficiently. Finding all odd divisors of a number up to 10^8 can be done by iterating up to sqrt(diff).

```python
import math

def get_odd_divisors(n):
    divisors = []
    if n == 0:
        return divisors
    # Find all odd divisors of n
    i = 1
    while i * i <= n:
        if n % i == 0:
            if i % 2 == 1:
                divisors.append(i)
            if i != n // i and (n // i) % 2 == 1:
                divisors.append(n // i)
        i += 1
    return divisors

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    a, b, c = map(int, input().split())
    diff = abs(a - b)
    
    # Get all odd divisors of diff
    odd_divisors = get_odd_divisors(diff)
    
    found = False
    result = -1
    
    for d in odd_divisors:
        n = 2 * diff // d
        # Check if all a, b, c are within range [1, n]
        if n < max(a, b, c):
            continue
            
        # Calculate who c is looking at
        # c looks at (c + n//2 - 1) % n + 1
        opposite = (c + n // 2 - 1) % n + 1
        result = opposite
        found = True
        break
    
    print(result)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Some number of people (this number is even) have stood in a circle. The people stand in the circle evenly. They are numbered clockwise starting from a person with the number $1$. Each person is looking through the circle's center at the opposite person.

A sample of a circle of $6$ persons. The orange arrows indicate who is looking at whom.

You don't know the exact number of people standing in the circle (but this number is even, no doubt). It is known that the person with the number $a$ is looking at the person with the number $b$ (and vice versa, of course). What is the number associated with a person being looked at by the person with the number $c$? If, for the specified $a$, $b$, and $c$, no such circle exists, output -1.


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 10^4$) — the number of test cases. Then $t$ test cases follow.

Each test case consists of one line containing three distinct integers $a$, $b$, $c$ ($1 \le a,b,c \le 10^8$).


-----Output-----

For each test case output in a separate line a single integer $d$ — the number of the person being looked at by the person with the number $c$ in a circle such that the person with the number $a$ is looking at the person with the number $b$. If there are multiple solutions, print any of them. Output $-1$ if there's no circle meeting the given conditions.


-----Examples-----

Input
7
6 2 4
2 3 1
2 4 10
5 3 4
1 3 2
2 5 4
4 3 2
Output
8
-1
-1
-1
4
1
-1


-----Note-----

In the first test case, there's a desired circle of $8$ people. The person with the number $6$ will look at the person with the number $2$ and the person with the number $8$ will look at the person with the number $4$.

In the second test case, there's no circle meeting the conditions. If the person with the number $2$ is looking at the person with the number $3$, the circle consists of $2$ people because these persons are neighbors. But, in this case, they must have the numbers $1$ and $2$, but it doesn't meet the problem's conditions.

In the third test case, the only circle with the persons with the numbers $2$ and $4$ looking at each other consists of $4$ people. Therefore, the person with the number $10$ doesn't occur in the circle.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

1. People stand in a circle with even number of people, numbered clockwise from 1
2. Each person looks at the person directly opposite them (through the center)
3. We're given that person `a` is looking at person `b`, and we need to find who person `c` is looking at
4. We need to determine if such a circle exists and output the result

## Key Insights

In a circle of `n` people (where `n` is even), person `i` looks at person `i + n/2 (mod n)`.

Since people are numbered from 1 to n, we need to be careful with modular arithmetic.

For person `a` to be looking at person `b`:
- If person `a` looks at person `b`, then `b = (a + n/2 - 1) % n + 1`
- This means `b - a ≡ n/2 (mod n)`
- So `(b - a) % n = n/2`

This gives us the key relationship: the difference between any two opposite people should be exactly `n/2` modulo `n`.

## Implementation

```python
def get_odd_divisors(n):
    divisors = []
    i = 1
    while i * i <= n:
        if n % i == 0:
            if i % 2 == 1:
                divisors.append(i)
            if i != n // i and (n // i) % 2 == 1:
                divisors.append(n // i)
        i += 1
    return divisors

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    a, b, c = map(int, input().split())
    
    d_abs = abs(b - a)
    
    # If d_abs is 0, but problem says distinct, so skip
    if d_abs == 0:
        print(-1)
        continue
        
    odd_divisors = get_odd_divisors(d_abs)
    
    found = False
    result = -1
    
    for m in odd_divisors:
        n = 2 * d_abs // m
        if n < max(a, b, c):
            continue
            
        # Check if all people are in the circle [1, n]
        if a > n or b > n or c > n:
            continue
            
        # Calculate who c looks at
        opposite = (c + n // 2 - 1) % n + 1
        result = opposite
        found = True
        break
        
    print(result)
```

def get_odd_divisors(n):
    divisors = []
    i = 1
    while i * i <= n:
        if n % i == 0:
            if i % 2 == 1:
                divisors.append(i)
            if i != n // i and (n // i) % 2 == 1:
                divisors.append(n // i)
        i += 1
    return divisors

t = int(input())

    d_abs = abs(b - a)
    
    # If d_abs is 0, but problem says distinct, so skip
    if d_abs == 0:
        print(-1)
        continue
        
    odd_divisors = get_odd_divisors(d_abs)
    
    found = False
    result = -1
    
    for m in odd_divisors:

        if n < max(a, b, c):

            continue
            
        # Calculate who c looks at

        result = opposite
        found = True
        break
        
    print(result)
